\operatorname{Tr}(\sigma,H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta})))=\operatorname{Tr}(\sigma,H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G^{\prime}},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta^{\prime}}))).

Let
g\in G(F^{\operatorname{nr}})
be as in Section
4.4.10
(b). Replacing
\phi
by
\phi\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g}
, we may assume that

\phi(\gamma)=\gamma^{\prime}
and there exists
h\in(G^{\operatorname{ad}}_{\gamma})^{0}(F^{\operatorname{nr}})\subseteq L((G^{\operatorname{ad}}_{\gamma})^{0})(k)
such that

\phi^{-1}\circ{}^{\sigma}\phi=\operatorname{Ad}_{h}
. Using the equality
{}^{\sigma}\phi=\sigma\circ\phi\circ\sigma^{-1}
, we conclude that

\operatorname{Ad}_{h}\circ\sigma=\phi^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\phi
.

Let
\phi_{*}:\overline{T}_{G}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\overline{T}_{G^{\prime}}
be the isomorphism induced by
\phi
(see Section
2.2.11
). By definition, for every admissible isomorphism
\varphi:\overline{T}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\overline{T}_{G}
, the composition
\phi_{*}\circ\varphi
is an admissible isomorphism
\overline{T}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\overline{T}_{G^{\prime}}
. Therefore we get an isomorphism
\phi_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta})\simeq\mathcal{L}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta^{\prime}}
, hence
\phi
induces an isomorphism

H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta})))\overset{\thicksim}{\to}H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G^{\prime}},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta^{\prime}})).

Thus the right hand side of (
5.19
) equals

\operatorname{Tr}(\phi^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\phi,H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta})))=\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Ad}_{h}\circ\sigma,H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta}))),

therefore it remains to show the equality

(5.20)

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Ad}_{h}\circ\sigma,H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta})))=\operatorname{Tr}(\sigma,H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta}))).

By Lemma
5.4.2
, there exists a finite increasing filtration
F^{r}
of
H_{j}(\widetilde{W}_{G},H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{st}}_{\theta})))
such that
L((G^{\operatorname{ad}}_{\gamma})^{0})
acts trivially on each graded piece
F^{r+1}/F^{r}
. Moreover, since for every
g\in L((G^{\operatorname{ad}}_{\gamma})^{0})
we have

\sigma\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{g}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\operatorname{Ad}_{\sigma(g)}
, the filtration
F^{r}
can be chosen to be
\sigma
-invariant. In this case, both sides of (
5.20
) equal

\sum_{r}\operatorname{Tr}(\sigma,F^{r+1}/F^{r})
.
∎

Appendix A
Generalized trace

A.1.
Construction

Let
L
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, let
A
be a finitely generated commutative algebra over
L
, and let
\psi\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
be an automorphism of
L
-algebra
A
of order
n
.

A.1.1
.

Notation.

(a) We denote by
B:=A\{u^{\pm 1};\psi\}
the ring generated by
A
and elements
u,u^{-1}
subject to
relations
u\cdot a=\psi(a)\cdot u
for all
a\in A
. Explicitly,
B
is a set of finite sums
\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{i}u^{i}
with
a_{i}\in A
and the product is given by the formula

(\sum_{i}a_{i}u^{i})\cdot(\sum_{j}b_{j}u^{j}):=\sum_{i,j}(a_{i}\cdot\psi^{i}(b_{j}))u^{i+j}.

(b) Notice that since
\psi^{n}=1
, we conclude that elements
u^{n},u^{-n}\in B
are central.

(c) We claim that
B
is a Noetherian ring. Indeed, this follows from the fact that

B
is finite over its commutative subring
A[u^{n},u^{-n}]
, and that
A[u^{n},u^{-n}]
is a finitely generated commutative algebra over
L
, thus Noetherian.

(d) Let
\langle u\rangle
and
\langle u\rangle_{n}
be the cyclic groups generated by

u
of infinite order and of order
n
, respectively, let
\langle\psi\rangle
be the (order
n
) cyclic group, generated by
\psi
,
and for every commutative ring
C
we denote by
C[\langle u\rangle]
and
C[\langle u\rangle_{n}]
the corresponding
group algebras.

(e) We denote by
B_{n}:=A\{u;\psi\}_{n}
the quotient ring
B/(u^{n}-1)
. Explicitly,
B_{n}
is the ring generated by
A
and element
u
subject to
relations
u^{n}=1
and
u\cdot a=\psi(a)\cdot u
for all
a\in A
.

(f) Let
A_{\psi}
be the quotient ring
A/\langle a-\psi(a),a\in A\rangle
. Then we the quotient map
A\to A_{\psi}
which induces quotient maps
B\to A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle]
and
B_{n}\to A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]
. Note that
\operatorname{Spec}A_{\psi}
is the scheme of fixed points
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}
. Therefore algebra
A_{\psi}
is finite-dimensional over
L
if and only if the scheme
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}
is finite.

A.1.2
.

K
-groups.
(a) For every
L
-algebra
R
, we denote by
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}
the category of
R
-modules, by

\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{R}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{R}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}_{R}
the subcategories of finitely generated
R
-modules and of
R
-modules, which are finite dimensional over
L
, and denote by
K(R)
and
K(R,\operatorname{fd})
the
K
-groups of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{R}
and
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{R}
, respectively.

(b) We denote by
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{B}
the subcategories of modules
M
, which are
u
-locally finite
, that is, unions of
u
-invariant finite dimensional
L
-vector spaces.

(c) Note that
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
is a tensor category with respect to the tensor product
-\otimes_{L}-
and that subcategory

\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{L[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}=\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
is a tensor subcategory.

(d) Note that
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
is a module category over
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
. Namely, to a pair
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
and
V\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
one associates a tensor product

V\otimes_{L}M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
such that
a(v\otimes m)=v\otimes a(m)
and
u(v\otimes m)=u(v)\otimes u(m)

for every
v\in V,m\in M
and
a\in A
. Furthermore,
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{B_{n}}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
are
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{L[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}
-module subcategories.

(e) We denote by
K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})
and
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})
the
K
-groups of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
and
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
, respectively. Moreover, it follows from part (c) that
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})
and
K(\langle u\rangle_{n})
are commutative rings and
that
K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})
and
K(B_{n})
are modules over
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})
and
K(\langle u\rangle_{n})
, respectively.

(f) Observe that the inclusion
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{L[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
induces a ring homomorphism
K(\langle u\rangle_{n})\to K(\langle u\rangle,u-\operatorname{lf})
, while the inclusions

\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{B_{n}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}

induce morphisms

K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])\to K(B_{n})\to K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})
of
K(\langle u\rangle_{n})
-modules, hence
a morphism

(A.1)

K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})\otimes_{K(\langle u\rangle_{n})}K(B_{n})\to K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})

of
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})
-modules.

(g) Notice that
K(B_{n})
and
K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])
can be interpreted as equivariant
K
-theories
K^{\prime}_{0}(\langle\psi\rangle,\operatorname{Spec}A)
and

K^{\prime}_{0}(\langle\psi\rangle,(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi})
of
\langle\psi\rangle
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
\operatorname{Spec}A
and
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}=\operatorname{Spec}A_{\psi}
, respectively.

Lemma A.1.3
.

The morphism (
A.1
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For every element
a\in L^{\times}
, we denote by
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},a}_{B}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
and
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd},a}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
the full subcategory consisting of modules
M
such that
a
is the unique generalized eigenvalue of
u^{n}
on
M
,
and let
K(B;a)
and
K(\langle u\rangle;a)
be the
K
-groups of
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},a}_{B}
and
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd},a}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
, respectively.

Note that since
u^{n}\in B
is central, every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
uniquely decomposes as a finite direct sum
M=\bigoplus_{a\in L^{\times}}M_{a}
, where

M_{a}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},a}_{B}
. Therefore the
K
-group
K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})
decomposes as
K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})\simeq\bigoplus_{a\in L^{\times}}K(B;a)
. Similarly,
we have a decomposition
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})\simeq\bigoplus_{a\in L^{\times}}K(\langle u\rangle;a)
, and morphism (
A.1
) decomposes as a direct sum of morphisms

(A.2)

K(\langle u\rangle;a)\otimes_{K(\langle u\rangle_{n})}K(B_{n})\to K(B;a).

Choose
b\in L
such that
b^{n}=a
, and let
V_{b}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
be the one-dimensional module over
L
and such that
u(v)=bv
for every
v\in V_{b}
.
Then the functor
-\otimes_{L}V_{b}
induces an equivalence of categories
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},1}_{B}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},b^{n}}_{B}
, and thus an isomorphism of
K
-groups
K(B;1)\overset{\thicksim}{\to}K(B;b^{n})=K(B;a)
and similarly an isomorphism
K(\langle u\rangle;1)\overset{\thicksim}{\to}K(\langle u\rangle;b^{n})=K(\langle u\rangle;a)
. Thus it suffices to show that morphism (
A.2
) is an isomorphism for
a=1
.

The latter assertion follows from the fact that the natural inclusion
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{B_{n}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},1}_{B}
induces an isomorphism of
K
-groups

K(B_{n})\overset{\thicksim}{\to}K(B;1)
and hence also an isomorphism
K(\langle u\rangle_{n})\overset{\thicksim}{\to}K(\langle u\rangle;1)
.

To see that the map between
K
-groups
K(B_{n})\overset{\thicksim}{\to}K(B;1)
is an isomorphism, we notice that every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf},1}_{B}
has a canonical filtration

0=M_{0}\subsetneq M_{1}\subsetneq M_{2}\subsetneq\ldots\subsetneq M_{n}=M,

where
M_{i}=\operatorname{Ker}(u^{n}-1)^{i}
. Since
B
is Noetherian, we have

M_{i+1}/M_{i}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{B_{n}}
for every
i
, and the inverse map
K(B;1)\to K(B_{n})
between
K
-groups is given by the formula
[M]\mapsto\sum_{i}[M_{i+1}/M_{i}]
.
∎

A.1.4
.

Example.
We have a ring isomorphism
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})\simeq\mathbb{Z}[L^{\times}]
, where
\mathbb{Z}[L^{\times}]
denotes the group ring of
L^{\times}
, such that

b\in L^{\times}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[L^{\times}]
is mapped to the class of character
L[\langle u\rangle]\to L:u\mapsto b
, and it induces an isomorphism

K(\langle u\rangle_{n})\simeq\mathbb{Z}[\mu_{n}]
. Moreover, for every
a\in L^{\times}
the subgroup
K(\langle u\rangle;a)\subseteq K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})
corresponds
to the
\mathbb{Z}
-span of
b\in L^{\times}
such that
b^{n}=a
.

Corollary A.1.5
.

Every group homomorphism
\Theta:K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])\to L
satisfying

(A.3)

\Theta(V\otimes M)=\operatorname{Tr}(u,V)\cdot\Theta(M)

for every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}
and
V\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{L[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}
uniquely extends to a homomorphism

\Theta:K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})\to L
satisfying the equality (
A.3
)
for every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
and
V\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
.

Proof.

Note that a homomorphism
\Theta:K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})\to L
satisfies equality (
A.3
) if and only if it factors through a localization
K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})_{u}
, where
u
is the prime ideal of
K(\langle u\rangle,\operatorname{fd})
, defined to be the kernel of the homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}(u,-)
.
By Lemma
A.1.3
, the natural homomorphism
K(B_{n})_{u}\to K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})_{u}
is an isomorphism.

Moreover, the localization theorem (see, for example,
[
Th
, Théoréme 2.1]
) together with the observation of Section
A.1.2
(g) implies that the natural morphism
K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])_{u}\to K(B_{n})_{u}
is an isomorphism. Therefore the composition
K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])_{u}\to K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})_{u}
is an isomorphism, and the assertion follows from the fact that
a homomorphism
\Theta:K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])\to L
satisfies equality (
A.3
) if and only if it factors through a localization
K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])_{u}
.
∎

Corollary A.1.6
.

Assume that the scheme
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}
is finite. Then
there exists a unique homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-):K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})\to L
such that

(i) for every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
and
V\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{L[\langle u\rangle]}
, we have an equality

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,V\otimes M)=\operatorname{Tr}(u,V)\cdot\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,M);

(ii) for every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{B}
, we have an equality

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,M)=\operatorname{Tr}(u,M)
.

Proof.

Since
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}
is finite, the algebra
A_{\psi}
is finite-dimensional over
L
. Therefore every

M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}
belongs to
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}
. Moreover, the usual trace map

\operatorname{Tr}(u,-):K(A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])\to L
satisfies equality (
A.3
).

Therefore it follows from Corollary
A.1.5
that
there exists a unique homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-):K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})\to L
, which satisfies condition (i) and condition (ii) for all

M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]}
. It remains to show that homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-)
satisfies
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,M)=\operatorname{Tr}(u,M)
for every

M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{B}
.

Let
I:=\operatorname{Ann}_{A}(M)
be the annihilator of
M
. Since
M\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fd}}_{B}
, we conclude that the quotient ring
A^{\prime}:=A/I
is finite-dimensional over
L
. Also since
M
is a
B
-module, we conclude that the ideal
I\subseteq A
is
\psi
-invariant, and
M
is induced from a
A^{\prime}\{u^{\pm 1},\psi\}
-module.

It suffices to show that the composition

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-)\circ\pi^{*}:K(A^{\prime}\{u^{\pm 1},\psi\},u-\operatorname{lf})\to K(A\{u^{\pm 1},\psi\},u-\operatorname{lf})\to L,

where
\pi:A\to A^{\prime}
is the projection, coincides with
\operatorname{Tr}(u,-)
.

Using Corollary
A.1.5
for
A^{\prime}
, we have to show that these two maps have the same restrictions to

K(A^{\prime}_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}])
. Since
A^{\prime}_{\psi}
is a quotient of
A_{\psi}
, this follows from the definition
of
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-)
.
∎

A.1.7
.

Remark.

Note that for every element
\lambda\in A^{\times}
there is an unique algebra automorphism
\phi_{\lambda}:B\overset{\thicksim}{\to}B
such that
\phi_{\lambda}(a)=a
for every
a\in A
and
\phi_{\lambda}(u)=\lambda u
. Therefore by Corollary
A.1.6
one can form a generalized trace map

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(\lambda u,-):K(B,\lambda u-\operatorname{lf})\to L,

which satisfies properties (i) and (ii) of Corollary
A.1.6
in which
u
is replaced by
\lambda u
. Note also that in general the subcategory

\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\lambda u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{B}
could be different from
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
.

A.2.
Asymptotic trace

A.2.1
.

Set up.
(a) We view
L[t]
as a graded algebra over
L
with
\deg t=1
, and let

\widetilde{A}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}A_{i}
be a commutative finitely generated graded
L[t]
-algebra such that

A_{0}
is finite-dimensional over
L
. In particular, each
A_{i}
is finite-dimensional over
L
,
and the action of
t
on
\widetilde{A}
induces a morphism
t_{i}:A_{i}\to A_{i+1}
of
A_{0}
-modules.

(b) For every
b\in L
we can form a quotient algebra
\operatorname{sp}_{b}(\widetilde{A}):=\widetilde{A}/(t-b)\widetilde{A}
, where
\operatorname{sp}
stands for
specialization
.
Note that
A:=\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{A})
is naturally isomorphic to the inductive limit
\operatorname{colim}_{i}A_{i}
with respect to the transition maps
t_{i}:A_{i}\to A_{i+1}
from part (a), where the canonical maps
\eta_{i}:A_{i}\to A
are compositions
A_{i}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{A}\overset{\operatorname{pr}}{\longrightarrow}A
.
Also
\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})
is a graded ring

\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})=\bigoplus_{i}\overline{A}_{i}
, where
\overline{A}_{0}=A_{0}
and
\overline{A}_{i+1}=A_{i+1}/t_{i}(A_{i})
for all
i>0
.

(c) Let
\widetilde{M}:=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}M_{i}
be a graded
\widetilde{A}
-module. Then it is a graded
L[t]
-module, so
the action of
t
on
\widetilde{M}
induces a morphism
t_{i}:M_{i}\to M_{i+1}
of
A_{0}
-modules. For every
b\in L
, we set

\operatorname{sp}_{b}(\widetilde{M})=\widetilde{M}/(t-1)\widetilde{M}
. Then
\operatorname{sp}_{b}(\widetilde{M})
is a
\operatorname{sp}_{b}(\widetilde{A})
-module, and
A
-module

M:=\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})
can be identified with inductive limit
M=\operatorname{colim}_{i}M_{i}
of
A_{0}
-modules. Again, each
\eta_{i}:M_{i}\to M
decomposes as

M_{i}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{M}\overset{\operatorname{pr}}{\longrightarrow}M
.

(d) Let
\psi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\widetilde{A})
be a (grading preserving) automorphism of order
n
as a graded
L[t]
-algebra. Then
\psi
induces automorphisms of algebra
A
and of a graded algebra
\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})
, hence of
\operatorname{Spec}A
and
\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A}))
. Then the construction of Section
A.1.1
applies, so we can form algebras
\widetilde{B}=\widetilde{A}\{u^{\pm 1},\psi\}
and
B=A\{u^{\pm 1},\psi\}
.

A.2.2
.

Remarks.
Suppose that we are in the situation of Section
A.2.1
.

(a) Since
\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{i}M_{i}
(see Section
A.2.1
(c)) and the functor of an inductive limit is exact, the functor
\operatorname{sp}_{1}:\operatorname{Mod}_{\widetilde{A}}^{\operatorname{gr}}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{A}
, where
\operatorname{Mod}_{\widetilde{A}}^{\operatorname{gr}}
stands for the category of graded
\widetilde{A}
-modules, is exact. Moreover,
this description of
\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})
also implies that
\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})\simeq 0
if and only if
\widetilde{A}
-module
\widetilde{M}
is
t
-torsion, that is,
t
acts on
\widetilde{M}

locally nilpotently.

(b) Notice that maps
\eta_{i}:A_{i}\to A
from Section
A.2.1
(b) gives rise to the homomorphism of graded

L[t]
-algebras
\eta:\widetilde{A}\to A[t]:\sum_{i}a_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i}\eta_{i}(a_{i})t^{i}
. Moreover, for every graded
\widetilde{A}
-module

\widetilde{M}
we have a morphism of graded
\widetilde{A}
-modules
\eta:\widetilde{M}\to M[t]:=A[t]\otimes_{A}M:\sum_{i}m_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i}t^{i}\otimes\eta_{i}(m_{i})
.
Arguing as in part (a) one sees that
\operatorname{Ker}\eta
is
t
-torsion.

(c) Notice that
t
acts injectively on
\widetilde{A}
(resp.
\widetilde{M}
) if and only if each
\eta_{i}:A_{i}\to A
(resp.
\eta_{i}:M_{i}\to M
) is injective
or, what is the same, if the homomorphism
\eta:\widetilde{A}\to A[t]
(resp.
\eta:\widetilde{M}\to M[t]
) from part (b) is injective. In this case,

\{A_{i}\}_{i}
form filtration of
A
in the sense of Section
A.4.1
(a) and
\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}A_{i}/A_{i-1}
is the graded ring of
A
.

(d) Notice that graded algebra
\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})
is finite-dimensional over
L
if and only if

\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A}))
is empty. Moreover, by observation of Section
A.2.1
(b) this implies that
A
is finite-fimensional over
L

or, what is the same, that
\operatorname{Spec}A
is finite. Furthermore, using part (c) the opposite implication holds if
t
acts on
\widetilde{A}
injectively.

A.2.3
.

Notation.
Suppose that we are in the situation of Section
A.2.1
.

(a) Denote by
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg}}_{\widetilde{B}}
be the subcategory of graded
\widetilde{B}
-modules
\widetilde{M}:=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}M_{i}
such that each
M_{i}
is finite-dimensional over
L
. Notice that every
\widetilde{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
is automatically
u
-locally finite, hence its quotient
M
is
u
-locally finite as well.
Therefore the exact functor
\operatorname{sp}_{1}
from Section
A.2.2
(a) gives rise to an exact functor

\operatorname{sp}_{1}:\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}\to\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
.

(b) Note that the subcategory
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd},t-\operatorname{tor}}_{\widetilde{B}}\subseteq\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
of
t
-torsion modules is a Serre subcategory (see
[
Stacks
, Tag 02M0]
),
so we can form the quotient abelian category
\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}:=\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}/\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd},t-\operatorname{tor}}_{\widetilde{B}}

(see
[
Stacks
, Tag 02MS]
).

(c) In particular, (see, for example,
[
Stacks
, Tag 02MX]
), we have an exact sequence on
K
-groups

K(\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd},t-\operatorname{tor}}_{\widetilde{B}})\to K(\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}})\to K(\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}})\to 0.

Lemma A.2.4
.

The exact functor
\operatorname{sp}_{1}:\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}\to\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}
from Section
A.2.3
(a) induces an equivalence of categories

\overline{\operatorname{sp}}_{1}:\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}_{B}.

Proof.

By Section
A.2.2
(a), we have
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd},t-\operatorname{tor}}_{\widetilde{B}}=\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{sp}_{1})
.
Therefore functor
\operatorname{sp}_{1}
induces a faithful functor
\overline{\operatorname{sp}}_{1}
(see
[
Stacks
, Tags 02MS and 06XK]
)

Next we claim that functor
\operatorname{sp}_{1}
is essentially surjective (hence
\overline{\operatorname{sp}}_{1}
is such), that is, for every
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{B}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}
, there exists
\widetilde{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
such that
\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})\simeq M
.

To construct
\widetilde{M}
, we choose generators
m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}
of
M
over
A
. For each
i
, we set
M_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{i}(u^{k}(m_{j}))\subseteq M
, where
A_{i}\subseteq\widetilde{A}
acts on
M
via projection
\widetilde{A}\to A
(see Section
A.2.1
(b)).
Then
\operatorname{colim}_{i}M_{i}\simeq\bigcup_{i}M_{i}=M
, each
M_{i}
is finite-dimensional over

L
, because
M
is
u
-locally finite, and
\widetilde{M}:=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}M_{i}

is a graded
\widetilde{B}
-module such that
\widetilde{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
(by construction),
and

\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{i}M_{i}\simeq M

(by Section
A.2.1
(c)).

It remains to show that functor
\overline{\operatorname{sp}}_{1}
is full, that is, is surjective on morphisms. Explicitly, we have to show that
for every two modules
\widetilde{M}^{\prime},\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
and every morphism

f:M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime\prime}
of
A
-modules such that
M^{\prime}=\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{1}(\widetilde{M}^{\prime})
and
M^{\prime\prime}=\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{1}(\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime})
comes from a morphism

\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{M}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}
in
\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
.

Recall that for every
\widetilde{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
the kernel of morphism
\eta:\widetilde{M}\to M[t]
from Section
A.2.2
(b) belongs to
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd},t-\operatorname{tor}}_{\widetilde{B}}
, hence the induced morphism

\eta:\widetilde{M}\to\eta(\widetilde{M})
in
\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
induces an isomorphism in
\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
. Thus, replacing

\widetilde{M}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}
by
\eta(\widetilde{M}^{\prime})
and
\eta(\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime})
, respectively, we can assume that
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}\subseteq M^{\prime}[t]
and
\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq M^{\prime\prime}[t]
.

Homomorphism
f
gives rise to homomorphism
f[t]=\operatorname{Id}\otimes f:M^{\prime}[t]\to M^{\prime\prime}[t]
of
B[t]
-modules (thus of
\widetilde{B}
-modules),
and we set
\widetilde{M}:=\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}+f[t](\widetilde{M}^{\prime})\subseteq M^{\prime\prime}[t]
. Then
f[t]
induces a homorphism
f[t]:\widetilde{M}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{M}
of
\widetilde{B}
-modules, and
the fact that functor
\operatorname{sp}_{1}
is exact implies that the embedding
\nu:\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{M}
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime})\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})
. Thus
\nu
induces an isomorphism in
\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
, and

\widetilde{f}:=\nu^{-1}\circ f[t]:\widetilde{M}^{\prime}\to\widetilde{M}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\widetilde{M}^{\prime\prime}

is a morphism we were looking for.
∎

Theorem A.2.5
.

In the notation of Section
A.2.1
, assume that the scheme of fixed points
\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A}))^{\psi}
is empty.

(a) For every
\widetilde{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
the sequence of traces
\{\operatorname{Tr}(u,M_{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
stabilizes,
so we can form the “asymptotic trace”

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\widetilde{M}):=\lim_{i}\operatorname{Tr}(u,M_{i}).

(b) Moreover, there exists a unique homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,-):K(B,u-\operatorname{lf})\to L
of abelian groups such that for every
\widetilde{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}}
we have
an equality

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M}))=\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\widetilde{M}).

(c) Furthermore, the scheme
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}
is finite, and the homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,-)
of part (b) coincides with the
homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-)
of Corollary
A.1.6
.

The proof of the theorem is based on the following particular case, which will be shown in the next subsection.

Claim A.2.6
.

In the notation of Theorem
A.2.5
(a), assume that
\widetilde{B}
-module
\widetilde{M}
is
t
-torsion. Then
\operatorname{Tr}(u,M_{i})=0
for each sufficiently large
i
.

A.2.7
.

Proof of Theorem
A.2.5
.

(a) The action of
t
on
\widetilde{M}
gives a graded
homomorphism
t:\widetilde{M}\to\widetilde{M}
of degree one, hence a
homomorphism
t_{i}:M_{i}\to M_{i+1}
for all
i
, commuting with the action of

u
. It suffices to show that for all sufficiently large
i
we have
\operatorname{Tr}(u,\operatorname{Ker}t_{i})=\operatorname{Tr}(u,\operatorname{Coker}t_{i})=0
.

Both
\operatorname{Ker}t=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{Ker}t_{i}\subseteq\widetilde{M}
and
\operatorname{Coker}t=\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{Coker}t_{i}
are graded

\widetilde{B}
-modules, which are killed by
t
. Moreover they are finitely generated because
\widetilde{M}
is finitely generated and
\widetilde{B}
is Noetherian,
so the assertion follows from Claim
A.2.6
.

(b) The assignment
\widetilde{M}\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\widetilde{M})
from part (a) is additive, so it gives rise to a homomorphism

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,-):K(\operatorname{Mod}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}})\to L
. By Claim
A.2.6
, we have
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\widetilde{M})=0
,
if
\widetilde{M}
is
t
-torsion, so by Section
A.2.3
(c), homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,-)
factors unique through

K(\overline{\operatorname{Mod}}^{\operatorname{fg},\operatorname{gr}-\operatorname{fd}}_{\widetilde{B}})
. The assertion now follows from Lemma
A.2.4
.

(c) Notice that we have isomorphisms

\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A}))^{\psi}\simeq\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})_{\psi})\simeq\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A}_{\psi}))

and
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(A_{\psi})\simeq\operatorname{Spec}(\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{A}_{\psi}))
. Therefore the first assertion follows from the observation of
Section
A.2.2
(d) applied to the graded algebra
\widetilde{A}_{\psi}
.

To show the second assertion, we have to show that the homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,-)
satisfies properties (i) and (ii) of Corollary
A.1.6
.
Property (i) follows from the fact that the assignment
\widetilde{M}\mapsto\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\widetilde{M})
from part (a) satisfies property (i), which is clear.
To show property (ii) notice that if
M
is finite-dimensional over
L
, then the graded module
\widetilde{M}:=\bigoplus_{i}M

satisfies
\operatorname{sp}_{1}(\widetilde{M})\simeq M
, in which case we have the equality
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,M)=\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{as}}(u,\widetilde{M})=\operatorname{Tr}(u,M)
.
∎

A.3.
Proof of Claim
A.2.6

The argument is based on the following lemma:

Lemma A.3.1
.

Let
Z
be a proper scheme over a field
L
equipped with an
action of a finite cyclic group
\Phi
with generator
\psi
such that
the scheme of fixed points
Z^{\psi}
is empty.

(a) For every
\Phi
-equivariant coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
Z
, we
have an equality

\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,H^{j}(Z,\mathcal{F}))=0.

(b) Assume that
Z
has an ample
\Phi
-equivariant line bundle

\mathcal{O}(1)
. Then for every
\Phi
-equivariant coherent sheaf

\mathcal{F}
on
Z
and every sufficiently large
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have an equality

\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,H^{0}(Z,\mathcal{F}(i)))=0
.

Proof.

(a) Thought the assertion is well known to specialists, we sketch the proof for
the convenience of the reader. The map
\mathcal{F}\mapsto\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,H^{j}(Z,\mathcal{F}))
is additive, so is gives rise to a homomorphism

K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z)\to L
from the equivariant
K
-theory of coherent sheaves on
Z
. By definition, this homomorphism can be written as the
composition of the pushforward
R\Gamma:K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z)\to K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,\operatorname{pt})=K[\operatorname{Rep}\Phi]
and the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,-):K[\operatorname{Rep}\Phi]\to L
. Thus it suffices to show that the composition
\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,-)\circ R\Gamma:K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z)\to L

vanishes.

Next we observe that the composition
\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,-)\circ R\Gamma
factors through the
localization
K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z)_{\psi}
, while by the localization theorem, we have

K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z)_{\psi}\simeq K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z^{\psi})_{\psi}
(see, for example,

[
Th
, Théoréme 2.1]
) . Since
Z^{\psi}
is empty, the group
K^{\prime}_{0}(\Phi,Z^{\psi})_{\psi}

vanishes, thus the composition
\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,-)\circ R\Gamma
vanishes as well.

(b) By a theorem of Serre, we have
H^{j}(Z,\mathcal{F}(i))=0
for all
j>0
and all sufficiently large

i
. Therefore part (b) follows from part (a).
∎

A.3.2
.

Set up.
(a) Let
A=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}A_{i}
be a commutative finitely generated graded algebra over
L
such that
A_{0}
is finite-dimensional over
L
, let
\psi\in\operatorname{Aut}(A)
be a grading-preserving automorphism of
A
of order
n
. Then
\psi
induces an
automorphism of
\operatorname{Proj}A
.

(b) Let
B_{n}=A\{u,\psi\}_{n}
be as in Section
A.1.1
. Then
B_{n}
is naturally a graded ring such that
A\subseteq B_{n}
is a graded subring and
\deg u=0
. Let
N=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}N_{i}
be a finitely generated graded
B_{n}
-module. Then each
N_{i}
is an
L[u]
-module finite dimensional over
L
, hence we can consider the trace
\operatorname{Tr}(u,N_{i})\in L
.

Corollary A.3.3
.

In the situation of Section
A.3.2
assume that the scheme of fixed points

(\operatorname{Proj}A)^{\psi}
is empty. Then for each sufficiently large
i
, we have

\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,N_{i})=0
.

Proof.

Assume first that algebra
A
is generated by
A_{1}
over
A_{0}
. Since
N
is a finitely generated

A\{u,\psi\}_{n}
-module, it is a
\Phi
-equivariant finitely generated
A
-module. Hence module
N

gives rise to a
\Phi
-equivariant coherent sheaf
\operatorname{Proj}N
on

Z:=\operatorname{Proj}A
. Moreover, for each sufficiently large
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have an isomorphism
N_{i}\simeq H^{0}(Z,\operatorname{Proj}(N)(i))
. Therefore the assertion
follows from Lemma
A.3.1
(b).

To deduce the general case from the particular case shown above, for each
d\in\mathbb{N}
we consider the graded subalgebra
A^{(d)}:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}A_{di}\subseteq A
.
Then there exists
d
such that the graded algebra
A^{(d)}
is generated by
(A^{(d)})_{1}=A_{d}
over
(A^{(d)})_{0}=A_{0}
(by
[
Stacks
, Tag 0EGH]
).
Also the inclusion
A^{(d)}\hookrightarrow A
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{Proj}A\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\operatorname{Proj}A^{(d)}
(by
[
Stacks
, Tag 0B5J]
).
In particular, the scheme of
(\operatorname{Proj}A^{(d)})^{\psi}
is empty.

Notice that
A
is integral and finitely generated (thus finite)
A^{(d)}
-algebra, thus
N
is a finitely generated
A^{(d)}
-module.
Moreover, module
N
decomposes as a direct sum
N=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{d-1}N^{(j)}
, where
N^{(j)}
is a graded finitely generated
A^{(d)}\{u,\psi\}_{n}
-module
\bigoplus_{i}N_{di+j}
. Moreover, since
A^{(d)}
is generated by
(A^{(d)})_{1}
,
it follows from the particular case shown above that for each
j
and each sufficiently large
i
we have
\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,N_{di+j})=0
.
Since the number of
j
’s is finite, we are done.
∎

The following simple criterion will be used later.

Lemma A.3.4
.

In the situation of Section
A.3.2
(a), assume that for every
i>0

there exists a basis
\mathcal{B}_{i}
of
A_{i}
over
L
such that

\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi^{j}(b)=0
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
.

Then the scheme of fixed points
(\operatorname{Proj}A)^{\psi}
is empty.

Proof.

Fix
\mathfrak{p}\in(\operatorname{Proj}A)^{\psi}
. Then for all
i>0
and
b\in\mathcal{B}_{i}
, we have

\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi^{j}(b)=0\in\mathfrak{p}
. Then we have
\psi^{j}(b)\in\mathfrak{p}
for some
j
(because

\mathfrak{p}\subseteq A
is a prime ideal), hence
b\in\mathfrak{p}
(because
\mathfrak{p}
is
\psi
-invariant).
This implies that
\mathcal{B}_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
for every
i
, thus
\bigoplus_{i>0}A_{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
,
a contradiction.
∎

Now we are ready to show Claim
A.2.6
.

A.3.5
.

Proof of Claim
A.2.6
.

Assume first that
t
and
u^{n}-1
act on
\widetilde{M}
as zero. Since
\widetilde{B}/(t,u^{n}-1)=\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})\{u,\psi\}_{n}
,
\widetilde{M}
is a finitely generated graded
\operatorname{sp}_{0}(\widetilde{A})\{u,\psi\}_{n}
-module, in which case the assertion was proven in Corollary
A.3.3
.

To deduce the assertion in the general case, we argue as in Lemma
A.1.3
:

Since
\widetilde{M}
is a
t
-torsion module over a Noetherian ring
\widetilde{B}
, it has a finite filtration
0=\widetilde{M}^{(0)}\subsetneq\widetilde{M}^{(1)}\subsetneq\ldots\subsetneq\widetilde{M}^{(n)}=\widetilde{M}
by graded submodules, where
\widetilde{M}^{(i)}=\operatorname{Ker}t^{i}
. Since the trace is additive, the assertion for
\widetilde{M}
follows from the assertion for all quotients
\widetilde{M}^{(i+1)}/\widetilde{M}^{(i)}
. Thus we can assume that
t
acts on
\widetilde{M}
as zero.

Next, since
\widetilde{M}
is finitely generated and
u
-locally finite, it decomposes as a finite direct sum
\widetilde{M}=\bigoplus_{a\in L^{\times}}\widetilde{M}_{(a)}
, where

u^{n}
has a unique generalized eigenvalue
a
on
\widetilde{M}_{(a)}
. Since it suffices to show the assertion for each
\widetilde{M}_{(a)}
, we can assume that
a
is the unique generalized eigenvalue of
u^{n}
on
\widetilde{M}_{(a)}
.

Moreover, as in the proof of Lemma
A.1.3
, choosing
b\in L
such that
b^{n}=a
and twisting, we reduce to the case
a=1
.
In this case,
\widetilde{M}
is
(u^{n}-1)
-torsion, thus arguing as in the second paragraph of the proof, we reduce to the case when
u^{n}-1
acts on
\widetilde{M}
as zero,
completing the proof.
∎

A.4.
Basic example

A.4.1
.

Rees rings and Rees modules.

(a) Let
A
be an
L
-algebra. By a
filtration
of
A
, we mean an increasing collection
A_{0}\subseteq A_{1}\subseteq A_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq A
of subspaces over
L
such that

1\in A_{0},\bigcup_{i}A_{i}=A
and
A_{i}\cdot A_{j}\subseteq A_{i+j}
for all
i,j
. To each such filtration
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
we associate its
Rees ring

R(A):=\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}t^{i}\subseteq A[t,t^{-1}]
.

Then
R(A)
is a graded
L[t]
-algebra on which
t
-acts injectively, and we have a natural isomorphism
R(A)/(t-1)R(A)\simeq A
.
Conversely, every graded
L[t]
-algebra on which
t
-acts injectively is isomorphic to a Rees ring
R(A)
corresponding to some
filtration of
A
(compare Section
A.2.2
(c)).

(b) In the situation of part (a) let
M
be an
A
-module, equipped with an increasing filtration
\{M_{j}\}_{j}
of subspaces over
L
. We say that
\{M_{j}\}_{j}
is
compatible with

\{A_{i}\}_{i}
, if for every
i
and
j
we have
A_{i}(M_{j})\subseteq M_{i+j}
.

In this case the Rees module
R(M):=\bigoplus_{i}M_{i}
is a graded
R(A)
-module on which
t
acts injectively, and
R(M)/(t-1)R(M)\simeq M
. Conversely, every graded
R(A)
-module on which
t
-acts injectively is isomorphic to a Rees module
R(M)
corresponding to some
filtration of
M
compatible with
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
.

(c) In the situation of part (b), we call a filtration
\{M_{j}\}_{j}

finitely generated
(over
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
) (also called
good
in other sources), if
R(M)
is a finitely generated
R(A)
-module. In this case,
M
is automatically finitely generated over
A
.

Conversely, the proof of the essential surjectivity of
\overline{\operatorname{sp}}_{1}
(see Lemma
A.2.4
) shows that if
M
is a finitely generated
A
-module, then
M
has a finitely generated filtration

\{M_{i}\}_{i}
over
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
.

A.4.2
.

Filtrations on monoids.

(a) Let
\Gamma
be a monoid, which in our application will be a
group. By a
filtration
of
\Gamma
, we mean an increasing
collection of subsets

\Gamma_{0}\subseteq\Gamma_{1}\subseteq\Gamma_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\Gamma
such that

1\in\Gamma_{0},\bigcup_{i}\Gamma_{i}=\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{j}\subseteq\Gamma_{i+j}

for all
i,j
.

(b) We will say that the filtration is
finitely
generated
, if in addition each
\Gamma_{i}
is finite,
and there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for each
i>m
we have

\Gamma_{i}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma_{j}\cdot\Gamma_{i-j}
. Note that in this case
the monoid
\Gamma
us generated by
\bigcup_{j=0}^{m}\Gamma_{i}
, thus

\Gamma
is finitely generated.

(c) Conversely, every finitely generated monoid
\Gamma
has a finitely generated filtration
\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{i}
.
Indeed, let
S:=\{\gamma_{1},....,\gamma_{n}\}\subseteq\Gamma
be a finite set of generators. Then the increasing collection of subsets defined by

\Gamma_{0}:=\{1\},\Gamma_{1}:=S\cup\{1\}
and
\Gamma_{i+1}:=\Gamma_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{1}
form a finitely generated filtration of
\Gamma
.
Moreover, if
\Gamma
is equipped with an automorphism
\psi
of finite order, then we can assume that
S
and hence also

\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{i}
is
\psi
-invariant.

(d) Let
L
be a field. Then a filtration
\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{i}
on a
monoid
\Gamma
gives rise to a filtration
\{L[\Gamma_{i}]\}_{i}
of its
monoid algebra
L[\Gamma]
, where
L[\Gamma_{i}]\subseteq L[\Gamma]
denotes the span of

\Gamma_{i}\subseteq\Gamma
over
L
.

(e) Note that if the filtration
\{\Gamma_{i}\}_{i}
is finitely generated, then the induced filtration

\{L[\Gamma_{i}]\}_{i}
of
L[\Gamma]
is finitely generated as well.
In particular, both
L[\Gamma]
and
R(L[\Gamma])
are Noetherian, if
\Gamma
is commutative.

A.4.3
.

Basic example.

(a) Let
\Lambda
be a finitely generated free
abelian group, let
\psi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda)
be an
elliptic
element
of finite order
n
, that is,
\Lambda^{\psi}=\{1\}
(compare Section
1.3.1
(d)), and let
\Lambda_{\psi}:=\Lambda/(\psi-1)\Lambda
be the group of
\psi
-coinvariants, which is automatically finite.

(b) Let
\langle u\rangle
and
\langle u\rangle_{n}
be the cyclic groups generated by

u
of infinite order and of order
n
, respectively, and let
\Delta:=\Lambda\rtimes\langle u\rangle
and

\Delta^{\prime}:=\Lambda\rtimes\langle u\rangle_{n}
be the semi-direct products, where
u
acts on
\Lambda
as
\psi
, that is,
u\lambda u^{-1}=\psi(\lambda)

for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

(c) We equip monoid
\Lambda
with a
\psi
-invariant finitely generated filtration

\{\Lambda_{n}\}_{n}
with
\Lambda_{0}=\{1\}
(see Section
A.4.2
(c)). It induces filtrations on
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}

such that
\deg u=\deg u^{-1}=0
.

(d) Let
L
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.

Then
A:=L[\Lambda]
is a commutative finitely generated algebra over
L
and the automorphism
\psi\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda)
induces an automorphism of
A
of finite order, thus putting us in the situation of
A.1.1
.

In particular, the algebra
B=A\{u^{\pm 1},\psi\}
is canonically identified with
L[\Delta]
, while

B_{n}=A\{u,\psi\}_{n}
is identified with
L[\Delta^{\prime}]
. Furthermore, by Section
A.4.2
(d)
the filtrations of part (c) gives rise to filtrations of
A
and
B
, thus to Rees algebras
\widetilde{A}=R(A)
and
\widetilde{B}=R(B)
.

Lemma A.4.4
.

In the notation of Section
A.4.3
(a), we have

(a)
|\Lambda_{\psi}|=\det(1-\psi):=\det(1-\psi,\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})
;

(b) element
u^{n}\in\Delta
is central, and
(\lambda u)^{n}=u^{n}\in\Delta
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

(a) Since
\psi
is elliptic, endomorphism
1-\psi\in\operatorname{End}(\Lambda)
is injective.
As
\Lambda_{\psi}
is the cokernel of the map
1-\psi\in\operatorname{End}(\Lambda)
, we get that

\Lambda_{\psi}
is a finite group of cardinality
|\det(1-\psi)|
. It particular,

\det(1-\psi)\neq 0
, and it remains to show that

\det(1-\psi)>0
.

Let
f_{\psi}(t)=\det(t-\psi,\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q})
be the characteristic polynomial of

\psi
. Since
\psi^{n}=1
, all roots of
f_{\psi}
are
n
-th roots of unity.
Since
f_{\psi}(1)=\det(1-\psi)\neq 0
, and

f_{\psi}(t)\in\mathbb{R}[t]
, we have a decomposition

f_{\psi}(t)=(t+1)^{k}\prod_{i}(t-\lambda_{i})(t-\overline{\lambda_{i}})
, therefore

\det(1-\psi)=f_{\psi}(1)=2^{k}\prod_{i}(1-\lambda_{i})\overline{(1-\lambda_{i})}>0
.

(b) Since
\psi^{n}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda)
is trivial, element
u^{n}\in\Delta
commutes with
\Lambda

and
u
, hence element
u^{n}\in\Delta
is central. Next for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
, its
n
-power

(\lambda u)^{n}
equals
\mu u^{n}
with
\mu=\lambda\psi(\lambda)\ldots\psi^{n-1}(\lambda)\in\Lambda
.
Moreover,
\mu\in\Lambda^{\psi}=\{1\}
.
∎

A.4.5
.

Remarks.
Assume that we are in the situation of Section
A.4.3
.

(a) Since
\Lambda_{\psi}
is a finite group, the algebra
A_{\psi}\simeq L[\Lambda_{\psi}]
is finite-dimensional over
L
. Thus the scheme of fixed points
(\operatorname{Spec}A)^{\psi}
is finite, hence the generalized trace homomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-)
is defined (see Corollary
A.1.6
).

(b) We claim that graded ring
\operatorname{gr}A
satisfies the condition of Lemma
A.3.4
, therefore the scheme of fixed points
\operatorname{Proj}(\operatorname{gr}A)^{\psi}
is empty, hence Theorem
A.2.5
applies. Indeed, each
\operatorname{gr}_{i+1}(A)=A_{i+1}/A_{i}
has a natural basis

\{[\lambda]\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{i+1}\smallsetminus\Lambda_{i}}
, and for every
\lambda\in\Lambda_{i+1}\smallsetminus\Lambda_{i}
, we have
\prod_{j=1}^{n}\psi^{j}(\lambda)\in\Lambda^{\psi}=\{1\}
, thus
\prod_{j=1}^{n}\psi^{j}([\lambda])=0
.

(c) Note that for every
\lambda\in\Lambda\subseteq A^{\times}
, every
u
-locally finite
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{B}^{\operatorname{fg},u-\operatorname{lf}}
is
u^{n}=(\lambda u)^{n}
-locally finite (see
Lemma
A.4.4
(b)), hence is
\lambda u
-locally finite. Thus the trace

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u\lambda,M)
is defined (see Section
A.1.7
).

(d) Note that the map
[\lambda]\mapsto[\lambda u]
defines a bijection

\Lambda_{\psi}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\Lambda_{\operatorname{adj}}\backslash\Lambda u
, where as before
\Lambda_{\operatorname{adj}}\backslash\Lambda u
is the quotient of the coset
\Lambda u\subseteq\Delta
by the adjoint
action of
\Lambda
.

Proposition A.4.6
.

In the situation of Section
A.4.3
, let
M
be a finitely generated
u
-locally finite
B
-module.

(a) For every
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we have an equality
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(\lambda u\lambda^{-1},M)=\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,M)
(compare Section
A.4.5
(c)).

(b) Each group homology
H_{i}(\Lambda,M)
is a finite dimensional representation of the cyclic group
\langle u\rangle
over
L
, and
we have an equality

(A.4)

\operatorname{Tr}(u,H_{*}(\Lambda,M)):=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}(u,H_{i}(\Lambda,M))=\sum_{\lambda}\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(\lambda u,M),

where
\lambda u
runs over a set of representatives of the
\Lambda
-conjugacy classes in
\Lambda u
(see Section
A.4.5
(d)).

Proof.

(a) Note that both homomorphisms
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,-)
and
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(\lambda u\lambda^{-1},-)
satisfy formula
(
A.3
). Thus, by Corollary
A.1.5
, it suffices to show the equality when
M
is a finitely-generated
A_{\psi}[\langle u\rangle_{n}]=L[\Lambda_{\psi}\times\langle u\rangle_{n}]
-module. In this case,
M
is finite-dimensional over
L
,
so we are reduced to the equality
\operatorname{Tr}(\lambda u\lambda^{-1},M)=\operatorname{Tr}(u,M)
.

(b) First we show that each
H_{i}(\Lambda,M)
is finite-dimensional. Arguing as in the proofs of Lemma
A.1.3
and Claim
A.2.6
, we reduce to the
case when
u^{n}
acts trivially on
M
. In this case,
M
is a finitely generated
A=L[\Lambda]
-module, in which case the finite-dimensionality
of
H_{i}(\Lambda,M)
is well-known.

Secondly we notice that it follows from part (a) that
\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(\lambda u,M)
only depends on the
\Lambda
-conjugacy class of
\lambda u
, thus
the right hand side of (
A.4
) is well-defined.

To show the identity (
A.4
), we notice that both sides are additive in
M
and satisfy formula
(
A.3
). Therefore, by Corollary
A.1.5
, we can assume that
M
is an

L[\Lambda_{\psi}\times\langle u\rangle_{n}]
-module, finite-dimensional over
L
.
Moreover, we can assume that
M
is irreducible,
that is,
M=M_{\xi}
corresponds to a character

\xi:\Lambda_{\psi}\times\langle u\rangle_{n}\to L^{\times}
. Twisting
M
by a character
of
\langle u\rangle_{n}
, we may assume that
\xi
is trivial on
u
, thus is a
character of
\Lambda_{\psi}
. In other words, we have to show that for
every character
\xi:\Lambda_{\psi}\to L^{\times}
, we have an equality

\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,H_{*}(\Lambda,M_{\xi}))=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\psi}}\lambda(\xi).

If
\xi
is non-trivial, then both sides of the last equation
vanish. So we can assume that
\xi
is trivial, in which case we
have to show the equality
\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,H_{*}(\Lambda,L))=|\Lambda_{\psi}|
.

But this follows from a well-known identity

\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,H_{*}(\Lambda,L))=\sum_{i=0}^{\operatorname{rk}\Lambda}(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}(\psi,\wedge^{i}\Lambda)=\det(1-\psi,\Lambda)

and Lemma
A.4.4
(a).
∎

Lemma A.4.7
.

In the situation of Section
A.4.3
, let
\Lambda^{0}\subseteq\Lambda
be a
\psi
-invariant subgroup, let
M^{0}
be a finitely generated
u
-locally finite
L[\Lambda^{0}\rtimes\langle u\rangle]
-module, and let
M=\operatorname{ind}_{\Lambda^{0}}^{\Lambda}M^{0}
be the induced representation.

Then we have an equality

(A.5)

\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(u,M)=\sum_{\mu}\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{gen}}(\mu^{-1}u\mu,M^{0}),

where the sum runs over a finite set of representatives
\mu\in\Lambda
of classes in
\Lambda/\Lambda^{0}
such that
\mu^{-1}u\mu\in\Lambda^{0}u
.

Proof.

Notice first that
\mu^{-1}u\mu\in\Lambda^{0}u
if and only if
\mu^{-1}\psi(\mu)\in\Lambda^{0}
. Therefore the finiteness of the sum follows from the fact that the automorphism
\psi
is elliptic, hence the induced map
\psi-1:\Lambda\to\Lambda
is injective, and hence the subgroup
(\psi-1)(\Lambda_{0})\subseteq\Lambda^{0}
is of finite index.

Next, notice that both sides of (
A.5
) are additive in
M^{0}
. Thus, arguing as in Proposition
A.4.6
(using Corollary
A.1.5
) we reduce to the case when
M^{0}
is finite-dimensional over
L
. Moreover, as we observed in Section
A.4.5
(b), Theorem
A.2.5
applies in our case.

As in Section
A.4.3
(c), choose a
\psi
-invariant finitely generated filtration
\{\Lambda_{i}\}_{i}
of
\Lambda
. Then
M_{i}:=\Lambda_{i}(M^{0})\subseteq M
gives a finitely generated filtration of
M
. Then vector space
M_{i}
decomposes as
M_{i}=\bigoplus_{\mu}\mu M^{0}
, where
\mu
runs over a set of representatives of the set of cosets
\Lambda_{i}\Lambda^{0}/\Lambda^{0}
, and we have an equality

\operatorname{Tr}(u,M_{i})=\sum_{\mu}\operatorname{Tr}(\mu^{-1}u\mu,M^{0}),

where the sum runs over the set of representatives
\mu\in\Lambda
of
\Lambda_{i}\Lambda^{0}/\Lambda^{0}
such that
\psi-(\mu)\Lambda^{0}=\mu\Lambda^{0}
, that is,

\mu^{-1}u\mu\in\Lambda^{0}u
. From this the assertion follows from Theorem
A.2.5
(c).
∎

Appendix B
Compatibility of actions (proof of Theorem
2.3.4
)

B.1.
The topological Jordan decomposition

B.1.1
.

Notation.
Let
H
be a connected linear algebraic
group over
K:=k((t))
, and let
\gamma\in H(K)
be a semisimple element.

We
say that
\gamma
is
bounded
(resp.
strongly semisimple
,
resp.
topologically unipotent
), if for every representation

\rho
of
H
over
\overline{K}
, each eigenvalue
\alpha\in\overline{K}^{\times}

satisfies
\operatorname{val}_{K}(\alpha)=0
(resp.
\alpha\in k^{\times}
, resp.

\operatorname{val}_{K}(\alpha-1)>0
).

B.1.2
.

Example.

Let
T
be a (split) torus over
k
. Then
\gamma\in T(K)
is strongly semisimple if and only
if
\gamma\in T(k)
.

The following result, which is called the
topological Jordan
decomposition
, is well known and widely used in the case when
K
is replaced by a

p
-adic field, but does not seen to appear in the literature in our (geometric)
setting.

Lemma B.1.3
.

Let
\gamma\in H(K)
be a bounded semisimple element.

(a) There exists a unique decomposition
\gamma=su=us
, called the topological Jordan decomposition, such that
s\in H(K)
is strongly
semisimple, and
u\in H(K)
is topologically unipotent.

(b) We have
H_{\gamma}=H_{s}\cap H_{u}
. Moreover, if
H
is connected, then
u,s\in H_{s}^{0}(K)
.

(c) An element
\gamma\in H(K)={L}H(k)
is strongly semisimple, if and only if the closure
of the cyclic group
\overline{\langle\gamma\rangle}\subseteq{L}H
is a diagonalizable algebraic group.

Proof.

(a) If
H=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, then the lemma asserts that for every

a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}=k[[t]]^{\times}
there exists a unique decomposition

a=su
, where
s\in k^{\times}
and
u\in 1+t\mathcal{O}_{K}
, which is clear.
Namely, for every
a=\sum_{i\geq 0}a_{i}t^{i}\in k[[t]]^{\times}
,
we have
a_{0}\in k^{\times}
and
a=a_{0}(a_{0}^{-1}a)
is the required decomposition.

To show the uniqueness of a decomposition in general, we choose a faithful representation

\rho:G\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(V)
of
G
. Extending
K
, we can assume that all
eigenvalues of
\rho(\gamma)
lie in
K
. Then
V
decomposes as a
direct sum
V=\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}
, where
V_{\lambda}
is the
eigenspace of
\rho(\gamma)
corresponding to
\lambda
. Since
\gamma
is
bounded, each
\lambda
belongs
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
. Since
s
and
\gamma

commute,
\rho(s)
stabilizes each
V_{\lambda}
. Now since
s
is
strongly semisimple, while
\gamma s^{-1}
is topologically
unipotent,
\rho(s)|_{V_{\lambda}}
has to be
\lambda_{0}\operatorname{Id}
.

To show the existence of a decomposition in general, we note that
\gamma
lies in a maximal torus

T\subseteq H
defined over
K
. Therefore we can replace
H
by

T
, thus assuming that
H
is a torus. By the uniqueness, we can
replace
K
by a finite Galois extension, thus assuming that
H

is split, thus
H\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
. Then we reduce to the case of

H=\mathbb{G}_{m}
, if which case the assertion was shown before.

(b) Note first that if
\gamma=su
is the topological Jordan decomposition of
\gamma
,
then
g\gamma g^{-1}=(gsg^{-1})(gug^{-1})
is the topological Jordan decomposition
of
g\gamma g^{-1}
for every
g\in H(K)
. Therefore we have an equality

H_{\gamma}=H_{s}\cap H_{u}
. It remains to show that if
H
is connected, then

u\in H_{s}^{0}(K)
. Since
\gamma
is semisimple, it lies in a maximal torus of
H
, which is connected, hence

\gamma\in H_{\gamma}^{0}(K)\subseteq H^{0}_{s}(K)
. Therefore by the uniqueness
of the topological Jordan decomposition, we conclude that
s,u\in H^{0}_{s}(K)
.

(c) As in part (a), we reduce the assertion first to the case of torus,
then to the case of a split torus, in which case the assertion is clear.

∎

Lemma B.1.4
.

Let
G
be a split connected reductive group over
K
,
\mathbf{I}\subseteq LG
an Iwahori subgroup, and

\gamma=su
the topological Jordan decomposition of
\gamma\in\mathbf{I}
. Then
s,u\in\mathbf{I}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{I}
has a presentation as a limit
\mathbf{I}\simeq\lim_{n}H_{n}
, where

H_{n}:=\mathbf{I}/\mathbf{I}_{n}
is an algebraic group over
k
and
\mathbf{I}_{n}\subseteq\mathbf{I}^{+}
. Then
\gamma\in\mathbf{I}
corresponds to a family
\gamma_{n}\in H_{n}
.
Let
\gamma_{n}=s_{n}u_{n}=u_{n}s_{n}
be the Jordan decomposition of
\gamma_{n}\in H_{n}
. By the uniqueness of the Jordan
decomposition, both
\{s_{n}\}_{n}
and
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
form compatible families, thus give rise to elements
s,u\in\mathbf{I}
such
that
\gamma=su=us
. It suffices to show that
s
is strongly semisimple, while
u
is topologically unipotent.

Since
u_{n}
is unipotent, we conclude that
u\in\mathbf{I}^{+}
, thus
u
is topologically unipotent. Next,
the kernel of each projection
H_{n+1}\to H_{n}
is unipotent, while each algebraic group
\overline{\langle s_{n}\rangle}\subseteq H_{n}

is multiplicative. Therefore each projection
\overline{\langle s_{n+1}\rangle}\to\overline{\langle s_{n}\rangle}
is an isomorphism.
Thus the projection
\overline{\langle s\rangle}\to\overline{\langle s_{n}\rangle}
is an isomorphism, hence
s\in\mathbf{I}\subseteq LG
is
strongly semisimple by Lemma
B.1.3
(c).
∎

Lemma B.1.5
.

Let
G
be a split connected reductive group over
K
, and
let
s\in LG(k)=G(K)
be a strongly semisimple element. Then

(a) the centralizer
G_{s}^{0}
is reductive group split over
K
;

(b) for every Iwahori subgroup
\mathbf{I}\subseteq LG
containing
s
, the intersection
\mathbf{I}\cap L(G_{s}^{0})
is an Iwahori subgroup of
L(G_{s}^{0})
.
Conversely, every Ihawori subgroup of
L(G_{s}^{0})
is obtained in which way.

Proof.

Though both assertions are well-known to specialists, we provide the proof for completeness. Since
G
is split over
K
, we can assume that
G
is defined over
k
.

(a) Note first that the statement is equivalent to the assertion that
s
is
G(K)
-conjugate to some element
s_{0}\in G(k)
. Indeed, assume that

G_{s}^{0}
is split over
K
, and let
T\subseteq G^{0}_{s}
be a maximal split torus over
K
. Since
G
is defined over
k
, there exists a split torus
T_{0}\subseteq G
over
k
. Hence there exists
g\in G(K)
such that
gTg^{-1}=T_{0}
. Then
s_{0}:=gsg^{-1}\in T_{0}(K)
is a strongly semisimple element, thus
s_{0}\in T_{0}(k)\subseteq G(k)
. The converse assertion is clear.

Now let us prove the assertion. Replacing
G
by
G^{\operatorname{ad}}
and
s
by its image under the projection
G\to G^{\operatorname{ad}}
,
we can assume that
G
is adjoint. Next, replacing
G
by
G^{\operatorname{sc}}
and
s
by its preimage under the projection
G^{\operatorname{sc}}\to G
, we can assume that
G
is simply connected, thus
G_{s}
is connected.

Moreover, since
G_{s}
split over a finite Galois extension
K^{\prime}
of
K
, the first paragraph of the proof implies that
s
is
G(K^{\prime})
-conjugate to some element
s_{0}\in G(k)
. We claim that
s
is
G(K)
-conjugate to
s_{0}
. It suffices to show that
H^{1}(K,G_{s_{0}})=1
. Since
G_{s_{0}}
is a connected reductive group and field
K
is of dimension
\leq 1
, the assertion follows from
[
BS
,
\lx@sectionsign
8.6, page 484]
.

(b) Note that since all Ihawori subgroups of
L(G_{s}^{0})
are
L(G_{s}^{0})
-conjugate, the second assertion follows from the first.

Fix a (split) maximal torus
T_{0}\subseteq G
over
k
. The assertion is clear in the case when
s\in T_{0}\subseteq L^{+}(T_{0})\subseteq\mathbf{I}
.
Since all pairs
(T,\mathbf{I})
, where
T\subseteq G
is a maximal torus split over
K
and
\mathbf{I}\supseteq L^{+}(T)
is an Ihawori subgroup, are

LG
-conjugate, it suffices to show that there exists a maximal
K
-split torus
T\subseteq G
such that

s\in L^{+}(T)\subseteq\mathbf{I}
.

We claim that there exists a subtorus
T^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
such that
s\in T^{\prime}
and the composition
T^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{I}/\mathbf{I}^{+}
is an isomorphism.
After this is done, the centralizer
T:=Z_{G}(T^{\prime})\subseteq G
is the maximal
K
-split torus we were looking for.

To construct
T^{\prime}
, we choose a presentation
\mathbf{I}\simeq\lim_{n}H_{n}
as in the proof of Lemma
B.1.4
, and let

s_{n}\in H_{n}
be the projection of
s
. Then
s_{n}\in H_{n}
is semisimple, so there exists a maximal torus
T^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq H_{n}
such that

s_{n}\in T^{\prime}_{n}
. Then the composition
T^{\prime}_{n}\hookrightarrow H_{n}=\mathbf{I}/\mathbf{I}_{n}\to\mathbf{I}/\mathbf{I}^{+}
is automatically an isomorphism. Moreover, we can assume that
the projection
\pi_{n}:H_{n+1}\to H_{n}
induces an isomorphism
T^{\prime}_{n+1}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}T^{\prime}_{n}
. Indeed, given
T^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq H_{n}
, we choose

T^{\prime}_{n+1}
be any maximal torus of
\pi_{n}^{-1}(T_{n})\subseteq H_{n+1}
containing
s_{n+1}
. Then
T^{\prime}:=\lim_{n}T^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
be the
torus we were looking for.
∎

B.1.6
.

Notation.

(a) Let
s\in G(K)
be a strongly semisimple element, thus the centralizer
G_{s}^{0}
is reductive group, split over
K

(by Lemma
B.1.5
(a)). Let
T\subseteq G_{s}^{0}
be a maximal torus split over
K
. Then
s\in T(K)=LT(k)
, thus
s\in L^{+}(T)
because
s
is bounded.

(b) Let
\mathbf{I}\subseteq LG
be an Iwahori subgroup such that
L^{+}(T)\subseteq\mathbf{I}
. Thus
s\in\mathbf{I}
, hence
\mathbf{I}_{s}:=\mathbf{I}\cap{L}(G_{s}^{0})
is
an Iwahori subgroup of
{L}(G_{s}^{0})
(by Lemma
B.1.5
(b)).

(c) Let
\widetilde{W}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{G}
be the affine Weyl group and the extended affine Weyl group of
G
, respectively
(see Section
2.1.2
), and let
\overline{\Phi}=\overline{\Phi}_{G,T},\widetilde{\Phi}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{G,T},\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}=\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{G,T,\mathbf{I}}
and
\widetilde{\Delta}
be the set of all roots, affine roots, positive affine roots and simple affine roots of
G
, respectively (see Section
2.1.7
).

(d) Let
\overline{\Phi}_{s}=\overline{\Phi}_{G_{s}^{0},T},\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{s}^{0},T},\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}^{+}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{G_{s}^{0},T,\mathbf{I}_{s}}
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
be the set of all roots, affine roots, positive affine roots and simple affine roots of
G_{s}^{0}
, respectively. By construction, we have inclusions

\overline{\Phi}_{s}\subseteq\overline{\Phi},\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}\subseteq\widetilde{\Phi}
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}^{+}\subseteq\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
.

(e) Let
\mathcal{A}_{G,T}
be the apartment, corresponding to
(G,T)
. Then every
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}
defines an affine function
on
\mathcal{A}_{G,T}
, and the Iwahori subgroup
\mathbf{I}
defines an alcove
A\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{G,T}
. Explicitly,
A
is the set of all

x\in\mathcal{A}_{G,T}
such that
\langle\alpha,x\rangle>0
for all
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
.

(f) Let
A_{s}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{G,T}
be the alcove, corresponding to
\mathbf{I}_{s}
. Explicitly,
A_{s}
is the set of all

x\in\mathcal{A}_{G,T}
such that
\langle\beta,x\rangle>0
for all
\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
.

(g) Let
\widetilde{W}_{s}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{G}
be the affine Weil group of
G_{s}^{0}
. Explicitly,
\widetilde{W}_{s}
is the subgroup, generated by simple affine reflections
\{s_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}}
. Finally, we set

\widetilde{W}_{G}^{s}:=\{w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}\,|\,w(\alpha)\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}\text{ for every }\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\}.

Lemma B.1.7
.

(a) For every
u\in\Omega_{G}
and
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
, we have
uw\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
.

(b) The subset
\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}\subseteq\widetilde{W}_{G}
is a set of representatives of the
quotient
\widetilde{W}_{G}/\widetilde{W}_{s}
.

(c) For every
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
, there exists an element

w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
such that
w(\alpha)\in\widetilde{\Delta}
.

(d) For every reduced decomposition
w=s_{n}s_{n-1}\ldots s_{2}s_{1}

of
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}\cap\widetilde{W}
, we have
s_{i}\ldots s_{2}s_{1}\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
for
each
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(e) For
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)
s_{\alpha}w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
; (ii)
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
; (iii)
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{s}
; (iv)
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}
.

Proof.

(a) Since for every
u\in\Omega_{G}
we have
u(\widetilde{\Phi}^{+})=\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
, the assertion follows.

(b) We have to show that for every
w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}
there exists a unique
w_{s}\in\widetilde{W}_{s}
such that
ww_{s}\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
.
Choose a point
x\in A
. Notice that
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
if and only if for every
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
we have

\langle\alpha,w^{-1}(x)\rangle=\langle w(\alpha),x\rangle>0
. In other words,
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
if and only if
w^{-1}(x)\in A_{s}
.

Since the affine Weyl group acts on the set of alcoves simply transitively, there exists a unique

w_{s}\in\widetilde{W}_{s}
such that
w^{-1}(x)\in w_{s}(A_{s})
. The last condition is equivalent to the condition that

(ww_{s})^{-1}(x)=w_{s}^{-1}w^{-1}(x)\in A_{s}
, that is,
ww_{s}\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
.

(c) First we claim that there exists a point
x\in A_{s}
such that
\langle\alpha,x\rangle<|\langle\beta,x\rangle|
for every

\beta\in\widetilde{\Phi}\smallsetminus\pm\alpha
. Namely, choose a point
y
such that
\langle\alpha,y\rangle=0
,
\langle\beta,y\rangle>0
for all
\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\smallsetminus\alpha

and
\langle\beta,y\rangle\neq 0
for all
\beta\in\widetilde{\Phi}\smallsetminus\pm\alpha
. Then every point
x
, which is close enough to
y
and such that

\langle\alpha,x\rangle>0
, satisfies the required property.

Choose
w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}
such that
w(x)\in A
.
We claim that
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and
w(\alpha)\in\widetilde{\Delta}
. Indeed, for every
\beta\in\widetilde{\Phi}
we have

\langle w(\beta),w(x)\rangle=\langle\beta,x\rangle
. Since
x\in A_{s}
, this implies that
w(\beta)\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
for every
\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
, hence
w(\alpha)\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
and

w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
. Moreover, by our assumption on
x
, for every
\beta\in\widetilde{\Phi}\smallsetminus\pm w(\alpha)
we have
\langle w(\alpha),w(x)\rangle<|\langle\beta,w(x)\rangle|
, which implies that
w(\alpha)
has to be in
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
.

(d) Assume that
s_{i}\ldots s_{1}\notin\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
. Then
s_{i}\ldots s_{1}(\beta)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
for some

\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\subseteq\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
hence
l(s_{i}\ldots s_{1}s_{\beta})<l(s_{i}\ldots s_{1})=i
.
Since
w=s_{n}\ldots s_{1}
is a reduced decomposition, we conclude that

l(ws_{\beta})<n=l(w)
, thus
w(\beta)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
, a contradiction.

(e) By definition,
s_{\alpha}w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
if and only if
s_{\alpha}w(\beta)\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
for
all
\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
. Since
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
we have
w(\beta)\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
.
Since
\alpha
is simple, it is the only positive root which
s_{\alpha}
sends to
negative. Therefore we conclude that
s_{\alpha}w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
if and only if

w(\beta)\neq\alpha
for all
\beta\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
, that is,
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
.

Similarly,
s_{\alpha}w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
if and only if
s_{\alpha}w(\beta)>0
for
all
\beta\in\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{s}
. Therefore the same argument shows that this happens
if and only if
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{s}
. Finally, for every
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
we get

w(\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}^{+})\subseteq\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
, thus
-\alpha\notin w(\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}^{+})
, hence

w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin-\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{s}
. Thus,
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{s}
if and only if
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}
,
and the proof is complete.
∎

B.2.
Application to affine Springer fibers

.

B.2.1
.

Set-up.

(a) Let
\gamma\in G(K)
be a bounded semisimple element, and
s\in G(K)

a strongly semisimple element such that
\gamma\in G_{s}^{0}(K)
.

(b) We set
G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}:=(G_{s}^{0})^{\operatorname{sc}}
, and let
\pi
be the
projection
G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}\to G^{0}_{s}\subseteq G
.

(c) Fix a maximal split torus
T\subseteq G
over
K
, and Iwahori subgroups
\mathbf{I}\subseteq LG
and
\mathbf{I}_{s}\subseteq{L}(G_{s}^{0})

as in Section
B.1.6
(a),(b).
Then the preimage
\mathbf{I}^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}\subseteq{L}(G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}})
of
\mathbf{I}_{s}
is an Iwahori subgroup as well.

(d) For every
w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}
choose representative
n_{w}\in N_{LG}(LT)
.

(e) Let
\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma}\subseteq\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}}
be the affine Springer fiber. Explicitly, it is the ind-scheme of all
g\in{L}(G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s})/\mathbf{I}_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}}
such that
g^{-1}\gamma g\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
.

(f) Note that our choice of
T
and
\mathbf{I}
induces an isomorphism
\zeta:\overline{T}_{G_{s}^{0}}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\overline{T}_{G}
. Then for every
\overline{w}\in\overline{W}
, we have an isomorphism
\zeta_{\overline{w}}:=\overline{w}\circ\zeta:\overline{T}_{G_{s}^{0}}\overset{\thicksim}{\to}\overline{T}_{G}
.

B.2.2
.

Example.
Let
\gamma\in G(K)
be a bounded semisimple element with the topological Jordan decomposition

\gamma=su=us
. Then
\gamma\in G_{s}^{0}(K)
(see Lemma
B.1.3
(b)), thus we are in the situation of Section
B.2.1
.

Lemma B.2.3
.

In the situation of Section
B.2.1
,

(a) for every element
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
, the map

g\mathbf{I}^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}\mapsto\pi(g)n_{w}^{-1}\mathbf{I}
defines a morphism
\eta_{w}:\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma}\to\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}
, which is independent of the choice of
n_{w}
;

(b) if either
s=1
or
\gamma=su
is the topological Jordan decomposition, then the induced map

\widetilde{\eta}:=\bigsqcup_{w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}}\eta_{w}:\bigsqcup_{w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}}\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma}\to\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}

is a universal homeomorphism;

(c) for every
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and
u\in\Omega_{G}
, we have
uw\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
, and morphism
\eta_{uw}
decomposes as

\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma}\overset{\eta_{w}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}\overset{u}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}
;

(d) for
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
with projection
\overline{w}\in\overline{W}
, the following diagram is commutative:

\begin{CD}\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma}@>{\eta_{w}}>{}>\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}\\
@V{\operatorname{red}_{\gamma}}V{}V@V{}V{\operatorname{red}_{\gamma}}V\\
\overline{T}_{G_{s}^{0}}@>{\zeta_{\overline{w}}}>{}>\overline{T}_{G}.\end{CD}

Proof.

(a) Since
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
, we have
w(\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}_{s})\subseteq\widetilde{\Phi}^{+}
, thus

(B.1)

n_{w}\pi(\mathbf{I}^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s})n_{w}^{-1}\subseteq n_{w}\mathbf{I}_{s}n_{w}^{-1}\subseteq\mathbf{I}.

This inclusion implies that the map

g\mathbf{I}^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}\mapsto\pi(g)n_{w}^{-1}\mathbf{I}
gives a well defined morphism
\eta_{w}:\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}}\to\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
. Moreover, if
g^{-1}\gamma g\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
, then

n_{w}(g^{-1}\gamma g)n_{w}^{-1}\in\mathbf{I}
, hence
\eta_{w}
restricts to a morphism

\eta_{w}:\operatorname{Fl}_{\mathbf{G}^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma}\to\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}
. Finally, morphism
\eta_{w}
is independent of the choice of
n_{w}
, because
\mathbf{I}\supseteq{L}^{+}(T)
.

(b) Since both
\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma}
and
\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}
are ind-proper ind-schemes, it suffices to show that morphism

\widetilde{\eta}
induces a bijection on
K
-valued points for every algebraically closed field extension

K\supseteq k
.

To show that
\widetilde{\eta}
is injective, assume that
\eta_{w}([g])=\eta_{w^{\prime}}([g^{\prime}])
for some elements
g,g^{\prime}\in LG^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}
. By definition, this means that
[\pi(g)n_{w}^{-1}]=[\pi(g^{\prime})n_{w^{\prime}}^{-1}]
, or, equivalently, that

n_{w}\pi(g^{-1}g^{\prime})n^{-1}_{w^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{I}
. Let
w^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{W}_{s}
be such that
g^{-1}g^{\prime}\in\mathbf{I}_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}}w^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{I}_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}}
. Using inclusion (
B.1
), we conclude that
n_{w}n_{w^{\prime\prime}}n^{-1}_{w^{\prime}}\in\mathbf{I}
, thus
ww^{\prime\prime}w^{\prime-1}=1
, hence
w^{\prime}=ww^{\prime\prime}
. Since
w,w^{\prime}\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and

w^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{W}_{s}
, we conclude from Lemma
B.1.7
(b) that
w=w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime\prime}=1
.
This also implies that
[g]=[g^{\prime}]\in\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}}
, thus
\widetilde{\eta}
is injective.

To show that
\widetilde{\eta}
is surjective, choose
[h]\in\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}
and a representative
h\in G(K)
of
[h]
. Then
h^{-1}\gamma h\in\mathbf{I}
, hence

\gamma\in h\mathbf{I}h^{-1}\cap L(G_{s}^{0})
. It now follows from Lemma
B.1.4
that
s\in h\mathbf{I}h^{-1}
. Hence, by Lemma
B.1.5
(b), the intersection
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}_{s}:=h\mathbf{I}h^{-1}\cap L(G_{s}^{0})
is an Iwahori subgroup of
L(G_{s}^{0})
, thus there exists
g\in G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}}(K)
such that
\mathbf{I}^{\prime}_{s}=g\mathbf{I}_{s}g^{-1}
. Then
g^{-1}\gamma g\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
, which means that
[g]\in\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma}
.
We claim that
[h]=\eta_{w}([g])
for some
w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}^{w}
.

Consider element
h_{1}:=h^{-1}\pi(g)\in G(K)
. Since
g\mathbf{I}_{s}g^{-1}=\mathbf{I}^{\prime}_{s}\subseteq h\mathbf{I}h^{-1}
, we conclude that
h_{1}\mathbf{I}_{s}h_{1}^{-1}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
,
hence
h_{1}L^{+}(T)h_{1}^{-1}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
. Therefore there exists
h_{0}\in\mathbf{I}
such that
h_{1}L^{+}(T)h_{1}^{-1}=h_{0}L^{+}(T)h_{0}^{-1}
,
hence
h^{-1}_{0}h_{1}\in N_{LG}(LT)
. Since
h_{0}^{-1}h_{1}=(hh_{0})^{-1}\pi(g)
, we can replace
h
by
hh_{0}
, thus assuming that

h_{1}=h^{-1}\pi(g)
equals
n_{w}
for some
w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}
. Then
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
because
n_{w}\mathbf{I}_{s}n_{w}^{-1}\subseteq\mathbf{I}
, and
[h]=\eta_{w}([g])\in\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{\eta}
.

(c) Since
uw\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
by Lemma
B.1.7
(a), the assertion follows from the definition.

(d) is straightforward.
∎

B.2.4
.

Notation.

(a) In the situation of Section
B.2.1
, for every local system
\mathcal{L}
on
\overline{T}_{G}
, we denote the local system
\zeta^{*}(\mathcal{L})
on
\overline{T}_{G_{s}^{0}}
simply by
\mathcal{L}
. Then it follows from Lemma
B.2.3
(d) that for every
w\in\widetilde{W}_{G}^{s}
we have a morphism

(\eta_{w})_{*}:H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\to H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}}).

(b) We denote the isomorphism

a_{w,\mathcal{L}}:H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\overset{\thicksim}{\to}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}})

from Proposition
2.2.7
simply by
w_{*}
.

Lemma B.2.5
.

Let
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
.

(a) Assume that
s_{\alpha}w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and
\gamma=su
is the topological Jordan decomposition. Then the composition

H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\overset{(\eta_{w})_{*}}{\longrightarrow}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}})\overset{(s_{\alpha})_{*}}{\longrightarrow}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{(\overline{s}_{\alpha})_{*}\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}})

equals
(\eta_{s_{\alpha}w})_{*}
.

(b) If
w^{-1}(\alpha)\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
, then the
following diagram is commutative:

\begin{CD}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})@>{(\eta_{w})_{*}}>{}>H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}})\\
@V{(s_{w^{-1}(\alpha)})_{*}}V{}V@V{}V{(s_{\alpha})_{*}}V\\
H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{(\overline{s}_{w^{-1}(\alpha)})_{*}\mathcal{L}})@>{(\eta_{w})_{*}}>{}>H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{(\overline{s}_{\alpha})_{*}\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}}).\end{CD}

B.2.6
.

Remark.

By Lemma
B.1.7
(e), exactly one of the assumptions
s_{\alpha}w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
and
w^{-1}(\alpha)\in\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
is satisfied.

Proof.

(a) We have to show that the action of
s_{\alpha}
on
\operatorname{Im}(\eta_{w})_{*}
is induced by the action by
n_{s_{\alpha}}
.
By the definition of the
\widetilde{W}_{G}
-action it remains to show that the image of

\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma}\overset{\eta_{w}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}\overset{\widetilde{\operatorname{red}}_{\gamma}}{\longrightarrow}\left[\frac{\mathbf{I}}{\mathbf{I}}\right]\to\left[\frac{B_{\alpha}}{B_{\alpha}}\right]

lies in the regular semisimple locus. Explicitly, we have to show that for every representative
g\in G^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}(K)
of
[g]\in\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma}
, we have
\alpha(\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbf{I}}(n_{w}g^{-1}\gamma gn_{w}^{-1}))\neq 1
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbf{I}}:\mathbf{I}\to\overline{T}_{G}
is the projection.
Notice that

\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbf{I}}(n_{w}g^{-1}\gamma gn_{w}^{-1})=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbf{I}}(n_{w}g^{-1}sgn_{w}^{-1})=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbf{I}}(n_{w}sn_{w}^{-1}),

and that element
n_{w}sn_{w}^{-1}\in T(K)
is strongly semisimple. Hence it remains to show that
\alpha(n_{w}sn_{w}^{-1})\neq 1
. Since
\alpha(n_{w}sn_{w}^{-1})=w^{-1}(\alpha)(s)
, we have to show that
w^{-1}(\alpha)\notin\widetilde{\Phi}_{s}
, but this follows from
Lemma
B.1.7
(e).

(b) Set
\beta:=w^{-1}(\alpha)
. The the assertion follows from commutativity of the diagram

\begin{CD}\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma}@>{\widetilde{\operatorname{red}}_{\gamma}}>{}>[\frac{\mathbf{I}_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}}}{\mathbf{I}^{\operatorname{sc}}_{s}}]@>{}>{}>[\frac{B_{\beta}}{B_{\beta}}]\\
@V{\eta_{w}}V{}V@V{}V{\operatorname{Ad}n_{w}^{-1}}V\\
\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma}@>{\widetilde{\operatorname{red}}_{\gamma}}>{}>[\frac{\mathbf{I}}{\mathbf{I}}]@>{}>{}>[\frac{B_{\alpha}}{B_{\alpha}}]\end{CD}

and the definion of the
\widetilde{W}_{s}
- and
\widetilde{W}_{G}
-actions.
∎

Corollary B.2.7
.

Assume that either
s=1
or
\gamma=su
is the topological Jordan decomposition. Then

(a) for every
w\in\widetilde{W}^{s}_{G}
, the composition

H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{sc},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\overset{\eta_{*}}{\longrightarrow}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\overset{w_{*}}{\longrightarrow}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}})

equals
(\eta_{w})_{*}
;

(b) For every
w\in\widetilde{W}_{s}
the following diagram is commutative:

\begin{CD}H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})@>{\eta_{*}}>{}>H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\\
@V{w_{*}}V{}V@V{}V{w_{*}}V\\
H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}})@>{\eta_{*}}>{}>H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\overline{w}_{*}\mathcal{L}}).\end{CD}

(c) The map
\eta_{*}:H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G_{s}^{sc},\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})\to H_{i}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G,\gamma},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{L}})
is
(\Lambda_{G_{s}^{\operatorname{sc}}}\times LG_{\gamma})
-equivariant.
Moreover, it is the
(\widetilde{W}_{s}\times LG_{\gamma})
-equivariant, if
\mathcal{L}
is
\overline{W}_{G_{s}^{0}}
-equivariant.

Proof.

(a) Assume first that
w
belongs to
\widetilde{W}
. In
this case, we will show the assertion by induction on
l(w)
. If

l(w)=0
, thus
w=1
, there is nothing to prove. Assume now that

l(w)>0
. In this case,
s\neq 1
, thus
\gamma=su
